# Fuel gap registry

**Every deferred, incomplete, or declined path in Fuel lives here — one greppable list, not scattered across 40 files.** This exists because the JIT launcher's scalar-only limitation was acknowledged in a code comment ("documented loader follow-up") but never on any schedule, so it rotted for months and shipped an all-zero output. A gap acknowledged in prose but not tracked is a leak.

## Rules

1. **Every** `todo!()` / `unimplemented!()` / `panic!(` on a non-test path, and every prose hedge, carries a `// GAP(GAP-NNN)` reference to a row below. **Enforced prose-hedge patterns (GAP-141 inc 2)**, in comments on production paths: `for now`, `not yet`, `doesn't yet`, `shortcut`, `bails`, `would need`, `stopgap`, `workaround`, `naive`, `temporary` (case-insensitive) + `TODO`, `FIXME`, `XXX`, `HACK` (uppercase markers) — **14 patterns**. A new hit fails CI unless GAP-referenced, reworded, or deliberately added to `fuel-ir/tests/gap_hedge_allowlist.txt` (which **may only shrink** — stale entries also fail, so removing a hedge forces removing its allowlist line). `todo!`/`unimplemented!` are enforced by inc 1 (`gap_refs.rs`); **`panic!(` is measured, not yet enforced — see GAP-016.** Baseline at adoption: 648 files, 221 hedges, 216-key allowlist, 0 new, 0 stale.
2. Adding such a path **without** a `GAP(...)` ref is a defect. **Enforcement is incremental and the gap between rule and gate is stated deliberately, because an unenforced rule 1 would be exactly the defect this registry exists to prevent** — see GAP-141. **Increment 1 (ratified 2026-08-07):** `todo!()` / `unimplemented!()` only, 36 sites. **Increment 2 (SCHEDULED, not deferred):** prose hedges via a shrinking allowlist — this is the increment that addresses the founding case, see GAP-141. **`panic!(` is measured but unenforced** pending a gap-vs-invariant policy — see GAP-016.
3. Close a gap → strike its row (`~~GAP-NNN~~ CLOSED <commit>`) and remove the `GAP(...)` ref from the code in the same change.
4. Owners: **A** = launcher / KISS-alignment / FKC-dispatch; **B** = cuda-backend / concurrency / gate; **C** = decode / models / fuel-core; **—** = pooled / unassigned. (Owners are roles, not sessions; claim by editing the row.)

## Tiers (completion order)

- **A — Correctness:** panics on production paths, silently-wrong results, missing gradients, races. Do these first; several violate the "never panic on production paths" hard rule.
- **B — Roadmap commitments / frontier:** architectural work other things depend on.
- **C — Feature completeness:** missing capability that currently **declines cleanly** (typed error / CPU fallback) — honest, lower-stakes.
- **P — Performance:** optimization only; no correctness stake.

| GAP-017 | `scripts/gpu-run.ps1` — DISCOVERABILITY, not the lock itself | A | **A sibling project has been hand-serializing GPU access for a problem that was solved 2026-08-01, and running UNGUARDED `cargo test` in the gaps.** Vulkane asked Fuel's coordinator to poll four Fuel sessions for an all-clear before its device-touching suites (2026-08-07), stating *"the lock protocol Fuel and Baracuda were going to land hasn't shipped"*. **It has.** `scripts/gpu-run.ps1` exists: `Global\gpu-run` machine-wide named mutex (line 76, commented "MUST be Global\ ... DO NOT VERSION"), `AbandonedMutexException` recovery that survives hard-kill/bugcheck (line 20), and **its own documented usage example at line 59 is `-Project vulkane -- cargo test --workspace`** — their exact case, written in as first-class. **Why this is Tier A:** unguarded concurrent GPU runs are what produced the 2026-07-31 host-aperture kernel bugcheck ([postmortem](../postmortems/2026-07-31-gpu-host-aperture-crash.md)); a peer who does not know the lock exists is an active recurrence risk, and a *second* project believing it must hand-coordinate means the belief may be general. **The defect is COMMUNICATION, not mechanism** — the lock works and was validated under a real contended multi-process kill. **Also note the anti-pattern in the request itself:** a human-mediated "is anyone using the GPU?" all-clear is **strictly worse than the mutex**, because it is a snapshot that can go stale between the answer and the run. Any peer asking for one should be handed the invocation instead. **SHARPER DIAGNOSIS (Vulkane, self-reported 2026-08-07) — this is NOT only a missed announcement.** Line 13 of `gpu-run.ps1` records it was **co-designed 2026-08-01 across fuel + baracuda + vulkane** — vulkane was *in the room*, then hand-serialized for six more days. Their account: a standing note said *"hold live GPU runs until Fuel+Baracuda's lock protocol lands, ping claude-peers to serialize by hand."* That note was **correct when written on 2026-07-31 and became wrong on 2026-08-01**, and it was acted on for six days without anyone re-evaluating its predicate. **The generalizable rule: a durable note that says "wait for X" needs a check for X, or it silently converts into permanent policy.** Corollary, in their words: *the recipients most likely to miss an announcement are the ones who already wrote down the old state, and they will look compliant the whole time.* An announcement alone therefore cannot fix this class — the fix is that "wait-for" notes carry an expiry check. **CAUTION, load-bearing given the Tier A framing (Lightbulb, 2026-08-07): the GPU lock is NOT a general flakiness fix.** GAP-019 is the worked counter-example from the same box the same day — a concurrent-*build* race the lock cannot touch. Anyone reading "Tier A GPU lock" and reaching for it against a superficially-similar flake will lose an hour. **Action:** confirm each GPU-touching sibling (baracuda, vulkane, lightbulb, kiss-ref) knows the lock shipped and how to invoke it cross-project; consider a line in each project's own CLAUDE.md rather than only Fuel's, since that is where the knowledge failed to reach. | OPEN |
| GAP-019 | build environment (shared box) — **not a Fuel source path**; mitigation belongs in `CLAUDE.md` | — | **RESOLVED SAME DAY: a concurrent-BUILD race, not a code defect. Fuel builds in the same environment and is exposed.** Lightbulb saw ~30 test binaries linking concurrently with rustc naming *different* dependency crates as "required to be available in rlib format" on each run, plus intermittent ICEs, while every target built and passed individually. **`cargo test --tests -j 4` passes completely** (614 lib tests + 25 integration targets, 0 failed, exit 0) on the identical tree that failed three times at default parallelism — this box gives cargo **~22 jobs**. **Five hypotheses falsified before the answer:** sccache, toolchain, stale artifacts, disk, and *Lightbulb-specific*. The fifth fell because **Fuel hit the same signature the same day**: Lightbulb's three dumps (`rustc-ice-2026-08-07T18_00_1{1,2}-*`, three within **two seconds**) lead with `delayed bug: no resolution for an import`, cascading through `path with Res::Err but no error emitted` and a run of `no type-dependent def for method call`; Fuel's lane-A worker independently reported the same leading string from two dumps in the `fuel-crash-vmm` worktree at 02:04/02:12. Surviving explanation: **concurrent rustc invocations against a shared build environment** — load-dependent, nondeterministic in *which* crates it names, and it **vanishes entirely when serialized**. **MITIGATION (adopt in Fuel): cap build parallelism.** `-j 4` is *known-good*, **not** a bisected boundary — the real threshold is un-measured, so do not treat 4 as "the limit". Note Fuel's existing rule is *"ONE cargo invocation at a time"*, which says nothing about `-j` **within** one invocation — that is the actual gap here, and a single `cargo test --workspace` at ~22 jobs trips it unaided. Pairs with GAP-017: different hazards, both fixed by not letting the box run flat out. **⚠️ EVIDENCE RETENTION — 5 occurrences today, 3 survive.** Fuel deleted two as untracked-file hygiene; Lightbulb deleted a fourth (`rustc-ice-2026-08-07T17_28_17-15204.txt`) about an hour before understanding what it was. Both checked the file was untracked and binned it anyway, for the same reason: **a stray file at a repo root reads as debris, and "untracked crash dump" is a category people tidy without thinking.** **Rule (Lightbulb's framing, adopted): it is not "never delete ICE dumps" — it is that a crash artifact is EVIDENCE until something explains it, so hygiene comes AFTER the explanation, not before.** Lightbulb's three are preserved outside the repo (verified on the bytes, not relayed) so a `git clean` cannot take them. **Owner: Lightbulb held the investigation and resolved it.** Fuel's remaining action is the `-j` cap. | **RESOLVED** (mitigation pending in Fuel) |
| GAP-018 | `fuel-vulkan-backend/src/lib.rs:146` (`buffer()`) | — | **The one unambiguous convention-only delay fuse found by the GAP-016 census (bucket 3).** `buffer()` **panics** when the storage has been **evicted to host**. The doc comment says to use `buffer_opt` instead — and **nothing enforces that**: no type distinction between an evicted and a resident handle, no `Result`, just prose. **Eviction to host is a real runtime state, not a hypothetical** — the Vulkan backend evicts under memory pressure — so this is reachable today by a caller who followed the type signature and not the comment. Tier A because it is a live never-panic violation on a production path, distinguishing it from GAP-047 (which needs a future code change to become reachable). **Fix, in preference order:** type-enforce the tier so an evicted handle cannot offer `buffer()` at all; else return `Option`/`Result`; else fault the storage back in. The first is the only one that makes the comment unnecessary. | OPEN |

If no, quantized prefill above batch 1 is *broken* on CUDA — a hard error, not a fallback. That distinction is exactly the "existence ≠ enforcement / correctly-named validator never invoked" pattern that has bitten Fuel repeatedly (see the `fkc-contract-verification-gap` memory), so **do not assume the fallback is wired because a comment says it should be**. **ANSWERED 2026-08-07 — it is BROKEN, not unoptimised.** Advertisement does not exclude `m>1` and **cannot**: (1) `BackendCapabilities` has **no shape/extent dimension at all** — the full struct is `backend_id`, `device_location`, `op_dtype_support: HashSet<(OpKind, DType)>`, `required_alignment`, `access_granularity_bits`, `transfer_paths`, `storage_substrate`, throughput rates; support is keyed on `(op, OUTPUT dtype)` only, so "M must be 1" is **structurally inexpressible** — not a forgotten registration, a type that cannot carry the constraint. (3) The bail text is *"only M=1 supported on CUDA today; got total_rows={total_rows}. Route prefill to Vulkan"* — an instruction to a caller with no mechanism to comply, and it computes `total_rows = batch * m`, so **batch>1 trips it even at m=1** — **CODE-GROUNDED, NOT MEASURED (labelled 2026-08-07 at the verifier's own insistence): the line was READ and its arithmetic is unambiguous, but no batched quantized decode was executed to observe the bail fire.** Cheap to confirm for whoever next holds a CUDA build: one realize of a batch-2 Q4_0 matvec. Kept distinct because this claim is already shaping a downstream consumer spec, and a read is a fact about the code while "the bail fires" is a fact about a run. (4) `pipelined.rs:798` is fail-fast by contract ("no runtime CPU fallback"), so the `Err` is terminal, not a retry. **Verdict:** an `Err` not a panic (no never-panic violation), but a **hard realize-time failure on a reachable path**, not a decline the optimizer routes around. The doc comment *was* the entire enforcement. **Structural root cause carved out to GAP-031.** **⚠️ THE SUGGESTED PROBE ABOVE TESTS THE WRONG AXIS — corrected 2026-08-08 by Lightbulb, and the correction is theirs about their own prior claim.** This row suggested confirming with *"one realize of a batch-2 Q4_0 matvec"*, i.e. probing the **batch** axis. **The consumer's actual failure is on the `m` axis at batch 1.** Lightbulb had recorded *"Lightbulb's runner builds `batch = vec![ctx]` (one request per batch), so GGUF at batch 1 is safe on CUDA — the wall is hit at the batching step, not the GGUF step."* **That conflates `batch = 1` with `m = 1`.** They verified their own side: `engine_model.rs:222` calls `model.prefill(&ids, &mut st)` with the **full encoded prompt in a single call**, so on every request `m = prompt_tokens` (6 for their test prompts, far more in practice) and **batch size never enters it**. **Consequence: a one-request-at-a-time server trips this on the FIRST forward pass of EVERY request. The wall is at the GGUF step, not the batching step** — so GAP-007 gates their GGUF work **outright**, rather than gating batching later as they had sequenced it. **`batch = 1` and `m = 1` coincide in the decode steady state — which is where most reasoning about a serving loop lives — and diverge exactly at PREFILL, which is the one place the arithmetic matters.** **MEASUREMENT COMMISSIONED as step 0 of GAP-029 (2026-08-08): one realize of a Q4_0 matmul at `m = 6`, `batch = 1`, on CUDA through `gpu-run`, with a positive build artifact required before either result is believed.** Requested by Lightbulb, who explicitly declined to treat the conjunction as established — **the `bail!` is a code read of `storage.rs`, not an observed failure**, and this row already carries that caveat because an earlier `batch > 1` claim was relayed to them over-firmly on the same basis. **Second time that discipline has caught something between these two projects.** **RE-TIERING HELD PENDING THE RESULT, deliberately not in anticipation.** Lightbulb verified their target path is CUDA (`src/model_fuel/device.rs:22` — CPU is the *degraded* fallback, not the target), so **if the bail fires this is a hard blocker on a named external consumer's target hardware**, not an internal optimisation gap — a different tier. | GAP-015 | `fuel-dispatch/src/dispatch.rs:6554` (`global_bindings`) ×; `GLOBAL_REGISTRY` | B | **COLD-START LOCK DEADLOCK on a PRODUCTION path — reclassified 2026-08-07, was filed as a test-harness hang.** Reproduced + classified by the owner: a true user-space lock deadlock, not contention (CPU **flat** 25.38s→25.39s over 135s; 45 threads all `ThreadState=5`, **44 on `WaitReason=37` `WrAlertByThreadId`** = the SRWLOCK/condvar/`OnceLock` primitive; 45th is the main thread joining). **Deterministic with a concurrency threshold:** passes `-j2/4/8/16`, hangs `-j24/32`, and at both hanging values **exactly the same 343 of 760 tests complete — byte-identical sets** (a race would jitter). **Two-party:** removing either `dispatch::` or `optimize::` clears it (each against a still-hanging control); keep-based bisect on `dispatch::` converged to 7 `global_bindings_registers_*_family_from_contract` tests, and the culprit *spans* the final split ⇒ several concurrent callers, not one test. **MECHANISM — CORRECTED BY MEASUREMENT 2026-08-07. It is NOT a two-lock ABBA; it is a SINGLE-lock recursive-read deadlock on `GLOBAL_BINDINGS`.** The earlier suspect (the `OnceLock` initializer calling `register_derived_gpu_caps` and taking a **write** lock on the separate `GLOBAL_REGISTRY`, an ABBA candidate) was the hypothesis held by *both* the owner and lane A, and an env-gated lock trace (`FUEL_LOCK_TRACE=1`) run on the deterministic reproducer killed it. At the block: **3 threads blocked wanting `BINDINGS.WRITE`, all in `extend_global_bindings` (`dispatch.rs:6618`)**; **~30 threads blocked wanting `BINDINGS.read`**; **zero threads inside the initializer**, with 20+ already holding `got BINDINGS.read` ⇒ **init had COMPLETED**. Since init is done, readers can only be blocked by a queued writer, and writers only by a reader that never releases — **both parties on the same `RwLock`**. `GLOBAL_REGISTRY` is not in the loop, which is *why* lane A's Party-2 search came back negative: **that negative is a real result, not a dead end.** Supported mechanism: std `RwLock` blocks new readers once a writer is queued (anti-writer-starvation), so a thread holding a read guard that requests a **second** read waits on a lock its own guard blocks. **Rule any static search for this needs:** `RwLockReadGuard` has a `Drop` impl, so NLL does **not** end it early — it lives to end of scope. **Explicitly NOT proven, do not inherit as fact:** (1) the trace does not log guard *drops*, so the three `got BINDINGS.read … later want BINDINGS.*` threads are equally consistent with sequential non-overlapping acquisitions — suggestive, not proof; (2) **the offending code site is not yet found** — the obvious candidate is refuted, since `pipelined_bridge.rs:795` and `:877` sit in *different* functions (`build_optimized_graph`@705, `dispatch_with_plan_retry`@807) so the first guard is dead by the second call. **Also refuted earlier and still refuted:** `SystemTopology::build` does not hold a registry guard across the bindings call (`topology.rs:419-427` scopes and drops before `:433`). **Why this is Tier A and not a gate nuisance (architect + owner, code-grounded 2026-08-07):** `global_bindings()` is a **production** entry point, not test-only. Verified **by enclosing `fn`** (see the method warning below): `fuel-core/src/pipelined_bridge.rs:795,877` in `build_optimized_graph` (the optimizer path); `judge/mod.rs:1233,1255,1274`; `cast_fusion.rs:51`; `fuel-dispatch/src/pipelined.rs:2051` in `order_for`; and — the strongest of them — **`pipelined.rs:2215`, the FIRST statement of `fn compiler_thread_body` (declared :2209), i.e. a background compiler thread whose opening act is to touch the `OnceLock`.** That is a concurrent cold-start caller present in production **by construction, not by workload shape**; `driver.rs:112`'s comment corroborates ("re-acquiring `global_bindings()`, so a background…"). Its own doc comment names "production callers picking up the global table". **The serializer that would have defused this does not exist:** the one eager `drop(global_bindings())` warm-up (`pipelined.rs:8494`, commented as forcing init "before we read it") sits inside `fn realize_with_optimized_route_empty_equals_arm0` (:8433) — **test-only**. No production eager-init was found. So the test suite at `-j24/32` is not the defect — it is the **first workload that fans out enough concurrent cold-start callers to expose it**, and concurrent cold start is precisely the multi-session serving profile (stated near-term priority). Treat as a latent production deadlock. **The corrected mechanism STRENGTHENS this:** the writer party is `extend_global_bindings`, which per-backend init paths call in production — so both sides of the deadlock exist outside tests. **METHOD WARNING — do not repeat this (architect's error, caught by the owner):** the first `#[cfg(test)]` in a file is **not** the test boundary. `pipelined.rs` has five (`58, 7219, 8722, 8725, 8739`) and its main test module is at **7219**; line 58 guards a single `use`. An earlier version of this row cited per-file "boundary" line numbers derived that way — they happened to be right for three files and the method is still unsound. **Resolve production-vs-test by enclosing `fn`, never by the first `cfg(test)`.** Still UNVERIFIED by that standard, and explicitly not to be assumed test-only: `jit_ingest.rs:2737,2979,3697`; `optimize.rs:1011,2080`; `plan.rs:2000`. **Symptom text corrected:** the old "32 tests stall (18 `optimize::*` + 14 `pipelined::*`)" is measured-wrong and understates it — 343/760 complete and 401 never report, but most of those **never started** (all workers parked; only ~44 can block at once). `ranker::` alone contributes 170 never-started, and those are pure-CPU. The suite stops dead. Interim gate: `--test-threads=1`. **DIAGNOSED 2026-08-07 — cause found, two independent instruments agree; not yet fixed.** `fuel-dispatch/tests/runtime_fusion_optimize_e2e.rs:84-90` already documents the hazard in prose: *"`adopt_runtime_fused` and `clear_runtime_fused_for_tests` WRITE the global binding table (`extend_global_bindings`), so a `global_bindings()` read guard must NOT be held across them — same-thread read-then-write deadlocks. Each optimize call below therefore scopes its own read guard and drops it before the next adopt/reset."* The lock trace matches it exactly. **The defect is that the discipline is HAND-MAINTAINED PER TEST — sound in isolation, unsound across CONCURRENT tests.** Thread A scopes its guard correctly and holds it briefly; thread B concurrently calls `adopt_runtime_fused` → a writer queues; A's next read blocks behind that writer (std anti-starvation); A never releases; B never gets the write. **Every individual test can be correct and the suite still deadlocks** — which is precisely why per-module runs and `-j≤16` pass while `-j24/32` does not. **Second incident on the same lock (pattern, not coincidence):** `runtime_fused_kernels.rs:415-424` documents a prior bug where `finalize()`'s `.expect()` inside `extend_global_bindings` **panicked while holding the write lock**, poisoning it for every later test in the binary. This is the "existence ≠ enforcement — seams beat vigilance" class: a correctly-stated invariant with nothing enforcing it. **Production reachability CONFIRMED by the comment itself: adopt runs on the G7 background thread in production**, so the writer genuinely exists outside tests. **FIX RULING (architect, 2026-08-07) — copy-on-write read path.** Three shapes were proposed; the ruling is the third. *(a) Enforce guard scope structurally* — better than a comment but still a rule whose violation reproduces this deadlock. *(b) Take `adopt_runtime_fused` off the global write lock* — **REJECTED, it does not fix the bug**: the trace's blocked writers are in `extend_global_bindings`, the per-backend **registration** path, so the deadlock stays reachable via backend init while appearing fixed. *(c) **CHOSEN — make the read path immune (copy-on-write), so readers never block behind writers.*** Rationale: it is Fuel's own idiom (immutable content-addressed structures with CoW/structural sharing — the same idiom the optimizer-architecture thread names for plan specialization), the profile is textbook read-mostly (reads on every optimize/dispatch/Judge/cast-fusion/compiler-thread; writes only on adoption + backend init), and it retires the **lock-poisoning** incident class too, since there is no long-lived write lock to poison. **Implementation: dep-free** — `RwLock<Arc<KernelBindingTable>>` with an accessor that clones the `Arc` and drops the guard *inside* the accessor, so no guard can escape and the recursive-read deadlock becomes unrepresentable; writers clone-mutate-swap. `arc-swap` only if profiling later shows the momentary read lock matters. **Snapshot semantics — the argument is STRONGER than "nice for replay" (owner, accepted 2026-08-07):** a reader holding an `Arc` snapshot sees a point-in-time table, so an optimize pass becomes a **pure function of (graph, table-version)**. Without that, a pass observing the table mutate mid-pass can produce a plan **not reproducible from its own inputs**, which breaks the **recipe-identity property (`base_map_hash` equality) that Tier-2 convergence depends on**. So CoW is not merely deadlock-avoidance with a determinism bonus — it repairs a latent reproducibility hole. *Stated honestly as unverified:* no path is known to depend on seeing a mid-pass adoption (adopt exists to affect *subsequent* passes), but that has not been exhaustively checked. **Empirical confirmation that the writers are NOT adopt-specific, and a correction with it:** a causal test removing every `runtime_fused`-named test still hangs (control 343 ok / HUNG; skip-runtime_fused 342 ok / HUNG). The earlier attribution of the writers to `adopt_runtime_fused`/`clear_runtime_fused_for_tests` was **over-attribution from the doc comment** — the doc names those two because they are the ones *that test* had to discipline, not because they are the only writers. This is why fix option (b) was rejected: it would have passed a reproducer built around adopt and left the production registration path deadlockable. **Instrumentation landed on main as its own commit `c9b9e991`,** blind spots documented with it (no guard-drop logging; "want" is ambiguous between initializer-block and read-block; dispatch globals only). **Weigh before building:** flag any path that depends on seeing a mid-pass adoption. **Precondition CONFIRMED by a bounded sweep (lane A, 2026-08-07):** `fuel-core/pipelined_bridge.rs:795` holds `bindings_guard = global_bindings()` **across the whole optimize call** (:804), so the recursive-read model requires no hole — a guard genuinely is held across a re-entrant region. **REFUTED in the same sweep, correcting lane A's own earlier lead:** `cast_fusion_predicate` is **NOT production-wired** (test-only); production optimize uses LoweringRule + FusionRule (`opt.rs:187-228`). **STILL OPEN:** the identity of the *second* `global_bindings()` read taken under the :795 guard — an optimize callee that ignores the passed-by-ref guard; candidates are `order_for` (`pipelined.rs:2051`), `compiler_thread_body` (:2215), Lowering/Fusion rule internals, and the residency/layout passes. **Note this does NOT block the fix:** option 3 makes a recursive read unrepresentable at *any* site, so the build can proceed while the specific site is still unnamed — but a sweep that comes back empty would mean the model has a hole, so record the result either way. **GATE MUST MATCH THE CHANGE KIND:** returning `Arc<…>` instead of `RwLockReadGuard<'static, …>` is a **type/signature** change touching every caller — `--lib` is a blind instrument. Gate `--all-targets` on every consuming crate, and under `--features cuda` and `--features vulkan` separately (feature-gated callers make a green default build prove nothing).  **BUILT 2026-08-07 @ `c95d140a`.** `global_bindings() -> Arc<KernelBindingTable>`; guard acquired+released inside the accessor, writers clone-mutate-swap. **GATE = the reproducer, not a proxy:** `-j32` **HUNG at 343/760 -> PASS 4.3s (744 passed / 0 failed)**; `-j16` passed before and after. Swept past the failure region (`-j1/24/48/64` all pass, `runtime_fusion_optimize_e2e` at `-j32/-j64` too). **Throughput control:** `-j1` 17.4s vs `-j64` 2.8s (6.2x) — a fix that accidentally serialized everything would also go green, so a pass alone was not sufficient evidence. **Regression test is BORN-RED-verified by construction, not asserted:** the new test was grafted onto *reverted* sources and observed to FAIL at 30.00s (watchdog on a real deadlock) vs 0.58s green. It reduces the deadlock to **one thread** — holding a snapshot across `extend_global_bindings()` was a *self*-deadlock under the old type — so it no longer depends on `-j24+` to reproduce. **WRINKLE THE HANDOFF NOTES MISSED, and it makes naive CoW WRONG:** `adopt_runtime_fused` requires its check-then-bind to be ATOMIC w.r.t. other writers (its own doc comment, `runtime_fused_kernels.rs:262-274`). A bare clone-mutate-swap breaks it — two threads racing the same region+kernel both see 'not bound', both register, and reproduce the duplicate-`KernelRef` panic; it would also lose updates outright. Fixed by serializing writers on a separate `BINDINGS_WRITER` mutex, so readers stay unblocked while the `RwLock` write guard covers only a pointer swap. That separation also **retires the second incident on this lock** (`runtime_fused_kernels.rs:415-424`): `finalize()`'s `.expect()` no longer panics under the write lock, so it cannot poison the read path, and the unwind discards the clone leaving the published table at its last good value. **Blast radius was far SMALLER than estimated** — exactly ONE signature named the guard type (`global_bindings` itself) and `&Arc<T>` deref-coerces everywhere `&RwLockReadGuard<T>` did, so all ~80 call sites compiled unchanged. Gated to the change kind anyway: `--all-targets` on fuel-dispatch AND fuel-core, under `--features cuda` and `--features vulkan` separately, all exit 0. **STILL OPEN (does not affect the fix):** the identity of the second `global_bindings()` read taken under `pipelined_bridge.rs:795`'s guard was never pinned — CoW makes a recursive read unrepresentable at *any* site, so it is now moot for correctness, but it was never found and that is recorded rather than quietly dropped. Snapshot semantics claimed as a reproducibility gain are argued, NOT exhaustively proven: no path is known to depend on seeing a mid-pass adoption. **ARCHITECT'S NOTE — the wrinkle is a defect in the RULING, and recording it only as an implementation detail would lose the lesson.** The builder framed it generously ("neither the ruling nor the handoff carried the invariant"). The sharper statement: **I ruled a SHAPE — copy-on-write — and a shape does not carry the properties the thing it replaces was providing by accident.** `adopt_runtime_fused`'s check-then-bind atomicity was written down as a **comment on the caller**, never as a property of the lock, so nothing in the ruling's field of view mentioned it. A session working faithfully from my notes would have written the clean two-line version, run the gate **I specified**, gotten green, and shipped a race — because the race needs two adopters of the *same* region and the suite does not reliably produce that. **The gate was correct and would not have caught it.** Generalizable, and it has no natural prompt: **when replacing a synchronization primitive, enumerate what callers were getting from it INCIDENTALLY** — the guarantees nobody wrote down as guarantees because the old primitive made them free. Same family as *existence != enforcement*: the invariant existed, as prose, and prose is not a mechanism. **THE CONTROL THAT MATTERED — framing CORRECTED BY ITS OWN AUTHOR, because the first write-up implied foresight they disclaim:** `-j1` 17.4s vs `-j64` 2.8s — **6.2x**. A fix that accidentally serialized everything would ALSO have gone green on the reproducer, so "it passes" was never sufficient evidence on its own. **But it was NOT designed as a false-pass guard.** `bzfevnl6` ran the wide thread sweep for *margin* — confidence `-j32` was not a fluke — and the 6.2x only registered as meaningful when they **read the numbers back**. They asked it not be recorded as "design a throughput control", because the next person would think they need foresight they do not need. **The weaker but far more usable lesson: when a fix works by changing CONCURRENCY BEHAVIOR, the pass/fail axis and the performance axis are DIFFERENT AXES, and a gate on one is blind to the other.** Checkable after the fact by anyone who looks at both numbers — which is the point. Pair that with the born-red-by-construction method used here — the regression test was grafted onto **reverted sources** and observed to fail at the 30s watchdog vs 0.58s green — and both directions are pinned by evidence rather than by assertion. | **FIXED** — built, gated on the reproducer, born-red verified. |

(2) treat unprovable residency as agreement -> disposition test fails - caught; (3) make the storage fingerprint **always agree** -> **NOTHING FAILED**. **Sabotage 3 is the finding, and it corrects our prediction rather than confirming it.** `incompatible_cache_swap_is_rejected_not_rebound` survives all three because its lever is `max_seq_len`, which `validity_for` rejects as *structurally* stale — so `rebind_kv` is never reached. **It was never going to become load-bearing when the implementation landed.** It tests a genuine but different claim (that `max_seq_len` stays structural rather than being delegated to the guard) **while its NAME promised guard coverage it never had.** We were right about the test and wrong about the mechanism, and the mechanism is what generalizes: **"will this test go red once X lands" is NOT answerable by reading it.** **The fix is a seam, not vigilance:** a second arm at the SAME `max_seq_len` with DOUBLE the `kv_heads` — nothing structural sees that, since `shape_key` describes the **model**, not the cache — so `validity_for` returns `AllocationChanged` and the guard is the only thing between wrong-extent storage and a splice into a baked graph; **plus an explicit precondition assert on that disposition, so it fails if it ever stops exercising the path it claims to.** **Two distinct generalizations:** (a) **a sabotage that catches nothing is a FINDING, not a wasted run** — #3 taught more than the two that failed; (b) sabotage 1 showed the disposition test **cannot see a wrong re-bind** (it asserts `Rebound` + alloc_id re-keying, both preserved by a *gutted* re-bind) — **only the logit oracle sees it.** Two tests that look mutually redundant covered **disjoint** things, the more dangerous shape, because it invites deleting one as a duplicate. Same family as the `sampled-token equality` scar, found by a better method. **INTENTIONALLY RED BRANCH (the lane START, not its state): `wip/gap-028-guarded-rebind` @ `d90caeee`** (pushed, NOT on main — main stays green). Its commit message opens `*** THIS BRANCH IS INTENTIONALLY RED. Do not "fix" the failing test. ***` and names which test fails and why, so the next owner does not mistake the specification for a defect. **CORRECTION — that caveat was right about the test and WRONG about the mechanism, and the correction is the more useful half.** It read: "passes vacuously because nothing re-binds yet; re-watch once the implementation lands." In fact the test was **never going to become load-bearing**: its lever is `max_seq_len`, which `validity_for` rejects as *structurally* stale, so `rebind_kv` is never reached at all. It tests a real claim — that `max_seq_len` stays a structural key rather than being delegated to the guard — while its **name** promised guard coverage its **lever** could not deliver. Found by **sabotage, not by reading**: three sabotages against the finished implementation, recompilation confirmed on each — gutting the re-bind failed the logit oracle at 1.032e-1, treating unprovable residency as agreement failed the disposition test, and **making the storage fingerprint always agree failed nothing.** That no-op run is what located the hole. *A sabotage that catches nothing is a FINDING, not a wasted run*: the two that fired only confirmed the tests work; the one that did not fire identified which test was decorative. Generalization worth more than the instance: **"this test will start covering X once Y lands" is a prediction, not a fact** — and it is not answerable by reading the test. Repaired with a second arm at the SAME `max_seq_len` and DOUBLE the `kv_heads`, which no structural field can see (`shape_key` names the MODEL, not the cache), plus an explicit precondition assert that `validity_for` returns `AllocationChanged` — so the test now **fails if it ever stops exercising the path it claims to**, a seam rather than a resolution to be careful. Sabotage 1 additionally showed the disposition test **cannot see a wrong re-bind at all** (`Rebound` + the alloc_id re-key both survive a gutted implementation); only the logit oracle can. Two tests that look mutually redundant while covering disjoint things is more dangerous than an obvious gap, because the apparent redundancy invites deleting one as cleanup — both sites now carry a NOT-substitutes comment naming the sabotage that proved it. GAP-029 and GAP-006/022/027 remain unstarted.) **Design result already earned — record even if the work moves: the guard's real obligation is RESIDENCY, not shape.** Shape was already implied by existing key fields (`max_seq_len`/`n_layers`/`cache_dtype`/`shape_key` → the baked `cache_shape`), whereas **device was not expressible in the key at all**; the honest carrier is `KvCache` recording its own `DeviceLocation` at construction (it already takes `&Device` in `with_capacity`, and `Storage` exposes no public residency accessor, so inferring device from storage would be fragile). **Note the pattern:** this is the *second* finding this session that a Fuel key/type cannot express a fact someone needed it to express — the other is GAP-031 (capability vocabulary has no extent dimension). Possibly one pattern, not two coincidences. **That prediction SURVIVED implementation** (and is now generalized as GAP-034): residency really was the only guard clause that could not be derived from the thing being swapped — `fuel_memory::Storage` exposes a backend *variant* but no device ordinal, and `VulkanStorage` records none at all, so a same-geometry cache on a **different GPU of the same backend** is invisible in the bytes. `KvCache` recording its own `DeviceLocation` really was the honest carrier, with `None` meaning REFUSED and never CPU-by-default.
